# Next Steps — Build Handoff

This is the live "you are here" for a worker picking up Mosaic. It points at the
next buildable work and the decisions that constrain it. When the work below is
done, update this file to point at what comes next.

**Last updated:** 2026-10-12 (**U6 is complete — pointer capture and drag shipped, and half the routing rule turned out to already exist under another name.** `UiSystem::pressed_` persisted across frames and gated the click on a same-button release; that *was* a capture rule without the name, so the slice opened by renaming it `captured_`. What was genuinely missing: hover ignored the press entirely (`ui_button.hpp` documented that as deliberate), and there was no drag event at all. **Capture means the captured button owns the pointer:** no other button hovers while a press is held, and the captured one is hovered only while the cursor is inside its own rect — so it un-highlights when you drag off and re-highlights when you drag back, which is the affordance that tells a user whether releasing here will fire it. That changes shipped behaviour, so the comment asserting the opposite is corrected rather than left to contradict the code. **Four new actions**, on the button's existing channel: `drag_start` fires on movement past `kDragThreshold` rather than on press — otherwise every click in the engine would also emit `drag_start` and `drag_end`, and no handler could tell a click from a one-pixel drag — then `drag` per moving frame, `drag_end` on release, `drag_cancel` when the pointer leaves. Only the drag actions carry `x`/`y`; `click` and the hover actions keep their exact `{entity, action}` shape, which three shipped handlers already read. **The two endings are distinguishable without new plumbing, and that is the design's one real insight.** The editor's provider deliberately drops `down` when the cursor leaves the viewport panel, so a drag cannot keep running while the user clicks in the Inspector — which means `down` false with `inside` true is a genuine release, and `down` false with `inside` false is the pointer leaving. Aborting a drag and completing one are different outcomes, and conflating them is where drag code goes wrong. **Probes found two real gaps and one equivalent mutant, and telling them apart mattered.** Dropping `last_at_ = at` from the drag branch survived, because the no-movement test stopped one frame after `drag_start` and so only ever compared against that position; a test that moves twice then holds still now covers it. A stale `dragging_` would make the next plain click emit a spurious `drag_end`, which the after-cancel test could not see because it counts `drag_start`s. But removing `dragging_ = false` from the *release* stayed green and is **not** a gap: the press edge already resets it, and between release and the next press `captured_` is nil so the drag branch cannot run. That line stays as defence in depth with its comment corrected to say it is not load-bearing — the same call `ui_hover_style.hpp` made for its `is_valid` guard, and the same reasoning. **One test also passed before its implementation existed**, which is a finding about the test rather than a free win: it gave the neighbouring button a negative `order`, so the neighbour was never topmost and would not have hovered with or without capture. Replaced with a disjoint neighbour at a higher order, which is the only arrangement where the press lands on A while B is genuinely topmost later. **Gate:** 1343/1343, both lints clean, all three entry points exit 0 and silent. **What tests cannot judge, and is queued for the next human pass:** the threshold's *feel*. `0.01` is normalized and therefore anisotropic — about 19px horizontally against 10px vertically at 1920x1080 — and the value is a guess until someone drags something. Both are recorded in the backlog rather than left implicit. **Next:** the UI pillar's remaining groups are U8–U11; the five standing decisions from the previous entries are unchanged.)

**Last updated:** 2026-10-12 (**A human verification pass caught the only defect this whole arc produced, and no test could have.** Seven manual checks over #245–#249, all seven passing — but the notes on two of them reported **two warnings where one was described**, and that turned out to be the real finding. The per-entity order lint and the pairwise conflict check were each firing correctly on the same action: one about an entity whose own two orders disagree, one about the pair that entity is half of. **Both were individually right, which is exactly why 1326 tests could not see it** — the duplication existed only at the seam where two correct things meet, and nothing is positioned to assert on a seam. **Fixed in #251:** `without_entities_in_conflict` drops a per-entity finding whose entity is already named in a pairwise conflict, so one authoring mistake earns one line, and the line kept is the more informative one. Matching BOTH ends of a pair is load-bearing there — `a` and `b` are assigned by entity value rather than by which one an author edited, so checking only `a` would still double-report about half the time, and which half would depend on entity ids. The precedence lives in its own function rather than inside either check, so each stays a pure independent answer to its own question and a reader asking "why didn't I get the other warning" finds it in one place. **The checklist page was also wrong, and that is the more transferable lesson.** It quoted ONE of the two lines the step emits as the expected output, so a correct result read as a surprise and had to be chased down with screenshots. The sting: the code comments written in that same session say plainly that both diagnostics run. **Familiarity is the cause** — you stop seeing output you have grown used to — which makes it a rule to write down rather than a slip to be more careful about. **The practice is now a registry skill rather than a habit of this session:** `human-verification-pass`, which went 1.0.0 → 1.2.0 in one evening, and every version came from using it rather than from thinking harder about it. 1.0.0 from building two pages; 1.1.0 from the expected-output mistake above plus per-item screenshot attachment, asked for mid-pass because evidence routed through chat arrives unanchored from the step that produced it; 1.2.0 from a cadence correction. **The cadence decision, which is the part that makes this affordable:** run a pass over an **arc**, not per change. A pass costs a human a context switch and a block of attention; a change costs them one line, and a reader asked too often stops reading carefully. Keep a running list of unverifiable claims between passes — that list is what a pass is assembled from, and without it the items that get lost are precisely the ones nothing else was checking. An arc that accumulates none needs no pass, which is a good sign rather than a missed step. **Where that leaves the project:** `main` at 92f029f, 1326/1326, both lints clean, all three entry points exit 0 and silent. The 🟢 actionable tier is empty. One 🟡 item (the offscreen HDR target, whose additive-blending trigger has not fired) and eight 🔴 items, none started. **Next:** the five decisions the previous entry lists still need a human, and none has become more answerable on its own. If the goal is to keep moving, **pointer capture/drag** is the strongest pick — U6's remaining third, implementable headlessly, and substantial enough to be worth the next verification pass.)

each was written before the code existed to contradict it.** Every one is corrected in place rather than left to mislead, and that correction is now the most valuable half of each slice. **A probe habit that paid for itself four times:** derive probes from the code, never from the assertion list. Two gaps in #244 (dropping either overlap axis survived because the one non-overlapping case separated its buttons on *both*; dropping the pair normalization survived because sequential entities already iterate in ascending order — so the test now destroys the first button to make swap-and-pop reverse them, which is the production hazard `ui_hit.hpp` documents), one in #241 (`std::sort` is incidentally stable below **17** elements on this toolchain, measured, so both stability tests use 20), and a pre-existing one in the panel pass that the same probe exposed. **A process hazard now fixed in the harness:** restoring a mutated source with `mv` preserves its mtime, so ninja keeps the MUTANT binary and a later full `ctest` reports bogus failures — in the probed area, which is exactly where they look real. Always `touch` after restoring. **Gate at close:** 1323/1323, dependency-rule and test-name lints clean, `mosaic run`, `run --window` and `editor` all exit 0 and silent on the shipped demo. **WHAT NEEDS A HUMAN, and why each is blocked on judgement rather than effort:** (1) **the `Ambiguous` conflict's noise level** — reporting a tied-order pair is defensible, since destroying an unrelated button can flip the click with nothing looking different, but nothing in this repository has two buttons so it cannot be judged here; flagged rather than tuned. (2) **`imgui_test_engine`** — the recorded `ImGuiFieldVisitor` gap was filed as "needs a headless ImGui context", which was too optimistic and is now corrected: a null-backend context is easy, but exercising a `DragInt`'s write-back needs a simulated multi-frame mouse drag against ImGui's internal active-item state. That is a new third-party dependency, so it is a decision, not a task. (3) **pointer capture/drag**, U6's remaining third — implementable headlessly, but its payoff only verifies under someone's hands. (4) **enum combo boxes** for `anchor`/`fit_mode`/easing — needs enum names on the reflection surface, and the widget half lands in gap (2). (5) **the offscreen HDR target** — carries a real design decision (where UI draws relative to the resolve) and a visual result no test can check. **Next:** pick one of those five with a human in the loop. There is no further unattended work in this area that would not be busywork.)

**Last updated:** 2026-10-12 (**UI — the pairwise order conflict check shipped, the correctness check the #240 lint was only standing in for.** Two overlapping `UiButton`s can be ranked one way by `UiButton::order`, which decides the click, and the other way by `UiPanel::order`, which decides what the user sees on top — so the click lands on a button nobody can see, with no symptom but the wrong thing happening. `find_order_conflicts` reports those pairs. Its prerequisite landed with #241: before that only the panel pass sorted, so "what the user sees on top" was not well defined. **The rects are shared, not re-derived, and that was the first design decision.** `UiSystem::candidates` flattened every button into a `HitCandidate`; the check needs the *same* rects, so the flatten moved to `engine/ui/ui_button_rects.hpp` and both use it. A diagnostic that resolved buttons its own way would police rectangles nobody clicks on, and would drift the first time either side changed. Deliberately **not** put in `ui_hit.hpp`, whose comment makes a point of needing no `World` and no component storage so `topmost_hit` stays trivially testable. **Two finding kinds, and the second one is a judgement call worth reviewing.** *Inverted* is the definite bug: both axes ordered, opposite rankings. *Ambiguous* is one axis tied and the other not — reported because the outcome then falls to iteration order over two different sparse sets, so **destroying an unrelated button can flip which one receives the click** with nothing in the scene having changed visually, exactly as `ui_hit.hpp`'s rule 2 warns. Nothing in this repository has two buttons, so I cannot tell yet whether *Ambiguous* proves useful or noisy in a real scene; it is flagged rather than quietly tuned. **Both axes tied is deliberately silent**, because the author expressed no preference either way and reporting it would fire on every pair of overlapping default-constructed buttons. **Only the four authored integers are compared** — the check never asks which entity comes first in a `for_each`, because that is precisely the fragile property it exists to warn about. **Overlap is inclusive**, matching `topmost_hit`'s `>=`/`<=` containment, so two buttons touching along an edge count: a pointer exactly on that line is inside both. **Probes: 9 of 9 red, and two were real gaps that each taught something.** Dropping *either* axis from `rects_overlap` survived, because the one non-overlapping case separated its buttons on **both** axes and stayed non-overlapping with half the test gone; the distinguishing inputs are a pair overlapping in x only and its mirror in y only, and both now exist. Dropping the pair normalization also survived, because entities created in sequence iterate in ascending order and the swap never fires — so I **measured** that destroying the *first* button makes the sparse set's swap-and-pop yield the later entity first, and built the test on that. That is the production scenario `ui_hit.hpp` documents, now pinned. **The ledger was unified rather than duplicated.** A pairwise finding has two entities and four numbers and does not fit `OrderWarningModel`'s key, so the transition-only semantics moved into a string-keyed `WarningLedger` that both diagnostics build their own key and signature for; `emit` returns indices so a caller maps results straight back to its own findings. Behaviour-preserving — all six existing cases pass unchanged, including the drag-burst reviewed at the window and kept. **A stale comment got corrected on the way through:** `order_warnings.hpp` argued "why every frame at all" from `order` *not* being editable in the Inspector, which #242 made false three commits after it was written. **Confirmed end to end, because the call site has no unit test:** a temporary second overlapping button in the demo produced both warnings — the per-entity lint on the one entity, the pairwise check on the pair — exactly once across 40 ticks, with the lower entity correctly normalized first. Reverted; the shipped demo has one button and stays silent. **Gate:** 1321/1321, both lints clean, all three entry points exit 0. **Nothing here needs eyes**, which is why this slice was chosen for unattended work. **What it still cannot prove:** that a reported conflict *causes* the wrong button to receive the event. That rests on `topmost_hit`'s tests plus the panel sort, covered separately but never together; a test driving a real pointer into a conflicting pair would close it and is filed. **Next:** combo boxes for the enum-as-int fields (needs enum metadata on the reflection surface); pointer capture/drag as U6's remaining third; the three duplicated anchor clampers, now recorded.)

That was unreachable until `field_int` shipped, so the two slices' interaction had never been seen by anyone; reviewed at the window, the verdict was to keep it. **Recorded in `order_warnings.hpp` itself, not only here**, because the next reader to watch seven lines appear during one drag will reasonably assume a de-duplication bug — the comment now says it is not one, that debouncing was considered and declined, and that the burst should not be "fixed" without revisiting the decision. Also confirmed: `field_int` edits reach the live component (dragging `anchor` moves the bar, 47 clamps to TopLeft without crashing, the scene goes dirty); the text pass sorts when two labels are made to overlap by hand; a negative `UiText::order` still does **not** cross the pass boundary, so the limitation written into both component headers is accurate; and headless `mosaic run` emits no `ui:` warnings, so the editor-time diagnostic has not leaked. **Nothing needed fixing.** The checklist is at https://claude.ai/artifact/SomnwAWoXWikiMKFbPbdqZ. **Still unverified anywhere:** `ImGuiFieldVisitor`'s widget calls remain untested by machine — T3 and T5 exercised them by hand, which is not a regression guard, so the recorded gap stands. **Next:** the real pairwise overlapping-button order check (its prerequisite landed with #241); combo boxes for the enum-as-int fields; pointer capture/drag as U6's remaining third.)

**Last updated:** 2026-10-11 (**Studio — `int` fields are editable in the Inspector, and a probe corrected a claim I had just written into the code.** This closes the gap the order lint opened two slices ago: a user read "UiButton order 7 but UiPanel order 0" in the Console and then could not edit either number, because `ImGuiFieldVisitor` had no `field_int` and every `int` rendered as a read-only label. Three small pieces: `FieldVisitor::field_int` defaulting to `field_readonly` (the same optional-hook pattern `field_asset`, `field_string`, `field_entity` and `field_handle_asset` all follow), one `std::is_same_v<U, int>` branch in `FieldVisitorAdapter`, one `DragInt` override. **Thirteen other `FieldVisitor` implementors needed no changes** — ten in tests, the `MarkdownFieldVisitor` behind `docs/reference/components.md`, three collectors in `mcp_tools.cpp` — because the default hook keeps them compiling, and the default renders `std::to_string`, byte-identical to the generic arithmetic branch it replaced. **Predicted and then verified: the generated component reference did not move** (`git status docs/reference/` clean after the pre-commit hook regenerated it), which mattered because that hook runs on every commit. **The payoff is wider than the lint that prompted it.** Surveying what `int` reaches made `ParticleEmitter`'s `burst`, `burst_trigger`, `max_particles` and three easing curves, `TrailRenderer::max_points`, `AudioSource::attenuation_model` and `NavAgent::goal_x`/`goal_y` editable too. **`int` only, deliberately** — the reflected `std::uint32_t` fields are ids, dimensions and versions (`Image::width`, `NetReplicated::id`, `AssetMetadata::version`), which nobody should drag. **Safety was checked rather than assumed, and all four enum-as-int paths defend themselves:** `to_anchor` clamps outside 0–8, `resolve_image_quad` falls back to Stretch, `apply_easing` and `to_ma_attenuation_model` each have a `default:` arm. The two allocation-shaped fields are safe too — `max_particles` is only ever compared (`<`), so a negative emits nothing, and `max_points` is guarded `> 0 ? : 1`. A `DragInt` also grants no new *capability*: `SetComponentCommand` already let a script write any of these. **The probe that mattered failed to compile, and that was the finding.** I had written a comment claiming the int branch sits below the bool branch because putting it above "would turn every reflected bool into a draggable 0/1". The mutant that does exactly that **does not build**: `field_int` takes `int&` and a `bool&` cannot bind to it, so the compiler forbids the mistake outright. The comment is corrected to say that, and the test's `ro:flag` assertion is relabelled as a cheap guard rather than the thing holding the line — it was never what made the ordering safe. Remaining probes 3 of 3 red (default hook made inert, the adapter handed a copy instead of the live field, the branch removed). **One gap recorded rather than papered over:** `ImGuiFieldVisitor` has **no test file at all** — pre-existing, not introduced here. ImGui needs a live context, so `field_float`, `field_string`, `field_asset` and now `field_int` are each one widget call no test executes. The adapter dispatch is well covered; what is not is "does the widget write back and set `changed_`". Filed, with the note that closing it needs headless-ImGui test infrastructure. **Gate:** 1296/1296, both lints clean, all three entry points exit 0 and silent. **Still unconfirmed by eye:** dragging an `order` in the running Inspector — the widget call itself is the untested seam above. **Next:** combo boxes for the enum-as-int fields (needs enum metadata on the reflection surface, probably its own spec); the real pairwise overlapping-button order check; pointer capture/drag as U6's remaining third.)

**Last updated:** 2026-10-11 (**UI — the HUD image and text passes now sort by `order`, and the backlog row describing the work was wrong in both of its claims.** The row called it "a copy of a shipped loop onto a sibling path" and said `order` was "reflected, documented, and ignored". **Reflected and ignored, yes. Documented, no** — `UiImage::order` and `UiText::order` carried **no comment at all**, unlike `UiPanel::order`; `docs/reference/components.md` is generated from reflection, so it listed the fields with no semantics. That turned out to be good news: with no promised contract to honour, the semantics were ours to define. **And it was not a copy.** The panel pass collects every quad and batches them into **one** `draw_ui` call after sorting; the image and text passes each draw **per entity**, carrying their own texture or font-atlas handle, so neither can be batched. Both needed a collect → `stable_sort` → emit **reshape**, which changes the order of the calls and not their number. All three passes now read alike. **The semantics chosen, and written into both headers:** higher `order` draws on top **within its own pass**; equal orders keep `for_each` order via the stable sort. **The pass boundary is absolute and the order cannot cross it** — the three passes run in a fixed sequence, so a `UiText` is above every `UiPanel` whatever either `order` says, and a negative `UiText::order` will **not** push a label behind a panel. That limitation is documented where a reader meets the field, not only in the backlog, and a unified cross-pass z-order is filed as its own architectural item. **The stability is load-bearing, not incidental:** `ui_hit.hpp`'s rule 2 documents the hit tie-break as agreeing with "the entity later in iteration draws on top", so an unstable sort here would quietly break a rule another file depends on. **Probes: 6 of 6 red, and the two interesting ones were about that stability.** Swapping `stable_sort` for `std::sort` survived at first — libstdc++ falls back to insertion sort below **17** elements, which is stable in practice, so three equal-order items cannot tell the two apart. Measured the boundary at exactly 17 and raised both stability tests to **20**; the probe went red. Then the same probe aimed at the **panel pass alone** came back green: **a pre-existing gap**, in the very pass the new comments cite as the reference rule. Closed it with a twenty-panel stability test, which is a cheap fix in code this slice was already touching. **The demo gained an overlapping sprite**, following U7's precedent of making a feature checkable by eye: a warm-tinted second `UiImage` at `order = -1` under the existing checker. Before the sort, `for_each` order would have put the later-created underlay on top — so the demo shows the fix rather than merely containing it. **A sandbox cannot screenshot the window, so the demo's claim is pinned by a test instead**: `tests/unit/app/demo_scene_test.cpp` renders the real default scene through a recording device and asserts the underlay is submitted first, identified by **tint** rather than index so a swap cannot pass. Confirmed load-bearing — removing the sort fails it. The new entity carries no `Transform`, like the existing `hud_label`, so the default-scene count assertion (which counts `Transform`s) is untouched. **Gate:** 1293/1293, both lints clean, all three entry points exit 0 and silent. **Still unconfirmed by eye:** the demo's overlap needs a human at the window; every claim a test can make is made. **Next:** `field_int` in the Inspector, which would make the previous slice's order warning actionable in-editor; the real pairwise overlapping-button check; pointer capture/drag as U6's remaining third.)

**Last updated:** 2026-10-11 (**UI — the `order`-disagreement diagnostic shipped, and building it proved two-thirds of its own recorded premise wrong.** The backlog asked for a warning on "an entity carrying both a `UiButton` and a `UiPanel`/`UiImage`/`UiText` whose `order` values differ", unblocked by the hover slice's ownership rule. That check was built, tested, and **fired a false positive on our own demo scene** — and the false positive was the valuable part. **The three HUD passes in `render_system.hpp` are fixed and sequential** (panels, then images over panels, then text over both) **and only the panel pass `stable_sort`s.** So a `UiText` is above a `UiPanel` unconditionally, whatever either `order` says: `UiImage::order` and `UiText::order` are reflected, documented, and **inert**. Comparing a button's `order` against either is comparing numbers that participate in no ordering at all. The demo made this unarguable: its `camera` entity carries `UiPanel{0}`, `UiImage{0}` and `UiText{1}`, so **no single button order could have silenced the warning** — the three visuals do not agree with each other either. **Scoped to button-vs-`UiPanel`**, which is the only comparison with a meaning, and the demo went silent. **The deeper correction, recorded rather than papered over: this is a heuristic lint, not a correctness check.** A single button cannot misroute a click whatever its `order` says. The real failure is **pairwise and relative** — two overlapping buttons where A outranks B in `UiButton::order` but B outranks A in `UiPanel::order`, so the click goes to A and the user sees B. The shipped lint catches the authoring slip that bug grows from (edit the panel's order, forget the button's), which is cheap; the real check needs rect-overlap testing between every pair of buttons and is filed as its own item. **A second premise also cracked:** the hover slice cited the template's entity 8 carrying button+image+panel+text as proof that co-location means ownership, but the demo piles the unrelated "MOSAIC" title and checker image onto `camera` purely for convenience. **Co-location is not always ownership** — the rule is a convention the repo follows, not an invariant the data enforces. Nothing breaks today, because the lint reads only `UiPanel`, which there genuinely is the button's visual. **The lint's advice is currently unactionable inside the editor**, and that is worth knowing before someone treats the warning as finished: `ImGuiFieldVisitor` has no `field_int`, so every `order` renders as a read-only label — a user reads "UiButton order 7 but UiPanel order 0" and then has to edit the scene file by hand. Filed as a quick win. **The de-dup is every-frame on purpose**, and the reason I gave for it while designing was wrong: `order` is not Inspector-editable, so "catches what a user types" is unreachable. It stands because `SetComponentCommand` goes through reflection, so a script, a plugin, the Script Console, an MCP `set_component` or the Blender live-link can all write an order mid-session. **Probes: 9 of 9 red, and the one gap found was real** — a comparison written on `std::abs` survived the first suite, because the only negative case (-1 against 0) has unequal magnitudes. The distinguishing input is **-2 against 2**: equal magnitude, opposite z, the worst disagreement to miss. A test for it exists and the probe is red. **A process hazard worth recording:** the probe harness restored each mutated source with `mv`, which preserves mtime, so ninja saw the file as up-to-date and **left the mutant binary in place** — a full `ctest` then reported four bogus failures. The harness now `touch`es after restoring. **Gate:** 1286/1286, both lints clean, all three entry points exit 0, and the editor is silent on the demo. **Functionally CONFIRMED end-to-end on 2026-10-11:** authoring `order = 7` on the demo's HUD button produced exactly one `[WARN] ui: entity ... has UiButton order 7 but UiPanel order 0` across 40 ticks — the call site and the de-dup together, which no unit test covers since the warn lives in `app/run/editor.cpp`. **Next:** the paired pass-sorting fix (image *and* text, now known to be one slice rather than the recorded image-only quick win); `field_int` to make this lint actionable; pointer capture/drag is still the remaining third of U6.)

**Last updated:** 2026-10-10 (**UI — engine-rendered hover feedback shipped, and the blocker it waited on turned out to be a non-problem.** Two backlog items were recorded as blocked on a missing definition of "this visual belongs to this button". **The relation already existed as ENTITY IDENTITY and had simply never been written down:** the shipped template's entity 8 carries `UiButton` + `UiImage` + `UiPanel` + `UiText` together, the demo adds `UiButton` to the `camera` entity that already holds the HUD `UiPanel`, and there is no counter-example anywhere in the repository. So the slice was "state the rule, then use it" rather than "design an ownership model" — worth remembering as a pattern, because the backlog's framing of a blocker was wrong in a way that made the work look bigger than it was. **The rule:** an element is styled by the nearest `UiHoverStyle` at or above it in the `UiParent` chain, and only if that chain reaches a `UiHovered`. **The walk goes UP** because `UiParent` points upward and nothing stores children — resolving a hovered button's descendants would have meant scanning every entity. Two things fell out free: the demo's nested "HP" label brightens with its parent bar, and a style authored on the bar is **inherited** by that label, so hover is configured once per button rather than once per visual. **`UiHovered` is deliberately unregistered, and that absence is load-bearing.** `ComponentReflection::save` iterates every *registered* type, so registering it would persist a phantom hover into any scene saved while the cursor rested on a button — and loading that scene would start up already highlighted, with no pointer near it and no exit event coming. Asserted two ways: a test that the type is not registered, and a test that serializes a world **while hovering** and finds no `UiHovered` in the output. The cost is that it does not appear in the Inspector, which works off reflection — a claim made while choosing the approach said it would, and the spec records that as withdrawn rather than quietly dropping it. **Feedback is a render-time override, never a mutation.** Nothing writes to `UiPanel::color` / `UiImage::tint` / `UiText::color`, so authored values survive. The demo previously assigned `panel->alpha` from a script; that worked and was the wrong thing to ship as the example, since a scene saved mid-hover would have persisted the tint. The script is deleted and the demo authors a style. **Multipliers rather than absolute colours**, so a default-constructed style is an exact no-op; the cost is that hover cannot be a *specific* colour, recorded as a backlog item. **The clamp is two-sided on purpose:** `tint` is authored data, so a scene can hold a negative, and a clamp written as `min(v, 1.0f)` passes that through to the shader as a negative channel. **Probes: 16 of 17 red, and the two that were not each taught something different.** One was a genuine coverage gap — mutating the cap-exhausted return left the suite green, because the only deep-chain case put the style ABOVE the cap where the collected value is null either way; the distinguishing input is a chain with a style INSIDE the cap and no tag anywhere, which that mutant would style while nothing is hovered. A test for it now exists and the probe is red. The other, dropping the `is_valid` guard in the walk, produced no red and **is not a gap**: `get_component` already returns nullptr for a nil or stale id because the sparse set compares the full `EntityID` including its generation. The guard stays as defence in depth, but the comment claiming it was what made nil and stale safe is corrected — that comment was the actual defect the probe found. One of the spec's eight named probes could not be built at all ("walk down instead of up" has no implementation, which is why the design walks up); probe 7, farthest-ancestor-wins, is the real equivalent. **Gate:** 1269/1269, tier rules and the test-name checker clean, and all three entry points exit 0 — including headless `mosaic run`, which installs no pointer provider, so nothing there can ever be hovered. **Visually CONFIRMED at the window on 2026-10-11:** hovering the HUD bar brightens **both the bar and its "HP" label**. That is the ownership rule confirmed at runtime, and it is the one claim no test could make — the label carries **no style of its own**, so its brightening can only come from inheriting the bar's style up the `UiParent` chain, through the render pass's own walk. The unit tests pin the walk and the arithmetic; only a human at the window could confirm the two meet correctly in a real frame. In `mosaic editor` it does nothing until **Play**, because the loop is stopped in authoring mode. **Next:** the `order`-disagreement diagnostic is now concrete and unblocked; pointer capture/drag is the remaining third of U6; 9-slice stays deliberately deferred as a longer-term goal.)

**Last updated:** 2026-10-10 (**Fix — click-to-select was dead on a freshly opened editor, a regression from the editor-starts-stopped slice that its own acceptance pass could not have caught.** `PhysicsSystem` creates backend bodies **lazily inside `update()`**, and `app/run/editor.cpp` calls `loop.stop()` while stopped, so `RuntimeLoop::advance` returns without ticking and `update()` never runs. Once the editor STARTED stopped, a fresh launch had **no physics bodies at all** — and `pick_entity` raycasts the physics world (`studio/pick.hpp`), so every viewport click cleared the selection instead of making one. Inspector edits to a `RigidBody`'s Transform were not reaching the backend either, by the same path. **Why the earlier 23-item manual pass missed it:** the editor started *playing* then, so the first tick created the bodies, and pressing Stop afterwards left them in place — the tester's click-to-select check ran against a world that had already ticked. **Why no test caught it:** every case in `pick_test.cpp` ticks first, and the setup comment says exactly what that buys (`// derive WorldTransform + register the body`), so the suite had no un-ticked case at all. **The fix:** `PhysicsSystem::sync_to_world` reconciles the backend with the ECS — reap, create, push Transform edits — **without advancing time**, and the editor calls it every frame beside the `transform_system.update(world, 0.0f)` it already called for the same reason. Deliberately **not** `update(world, 0.0f)`: that still reaches `backend_->step(0, substeps)` and then harvests contact events, so a stopped editor would emit contact-begin events for anything already touching, which the demo logs. Verified: a stopped editor run emits zero contact lines. The call is unconditional rather than gated on `!is_playing()`, because while playing `world.tick` runs the same reconcile as its first phase, so it is idempotent — and a gate there is one more thing to get wrong at the play/stop boundary. **The general lesson, worth more than the fix:** anything initialised lazily inside a system's `update()` silently does not exist in a stopped editor. Physics was the one that bit; any future system with lazy setup will behave the same way, and the editor is now a first-class caller that needs an explicit authoring-time entry point rather than a tick. **Gate:** 1213/1213 with two new cases — one picking on a frame that never ticked, one asserting the sync emits no contacts — plus both CI checkers clean and `mosaic editor` exiting 0.)

**Last updated:** 2026-10-10 (**UI — U6 interaction depth shipped, two of its three features: z-order hit resolution and hover enter/exit. The motivating bug was a WRONG answer, not an undefined tie-break.** `UiSystem`'s press path took the **first** button `for_each` yielded, while the panel pass `stable_sort`s ascending and draws in that order — so among equal `order` values the entity drawn **last** is on top. First-hit and last-drawn are opposite ends of the same list, which means a click on two overlapping equal-order buttons went to the button **the user could not see**. Worth recording that an earlier scoping read of `ui_system.hpp` concluded the opposite — that the *last* match won — and was corrected in the spec rather than quietly dropped, because it changes how strong the motivation is. **What shipped:** all the tie-break arithmetic in one pure function (`engine/ui/ui_hit.hpp`'s `topmost_hit`), testable with no world and no pointer provider; `UiButton` gains its own `order`, declared **last** so the demo's positional brace init kept compiling; and hover emits on the button's **existing** `event` channel with `action:"hover_enter"` / `"hover_exit"`, needing no new component field and no second channel because the click path already carried `{entity, action}`. **Why the maths is a pure function, third slice running:** the failure mode is picking the *wrong* button, which produces plausible behaviour — a click does something, just not the thing under the cursor — so neither review nor eyeballing catches it. **Revisit that seam if** hit resolution ever needs the hierarchy (U9 clipping regions, or ownership-aware ordering); today it is flat. **Capture/drag was split out at the start**, not dropped: z-order and hover are coupled ("which button is hovered" has no answer without a tie-break, so hover would have flickered between overlaps), while capture is already half-present and is the riskiest third — its failure modes are the subtlest, which is the bug class that shipped viewport pointer input **dead** while thirteen tests stayed green. **Two accepted costs, both recorded as live backlog items rather than footnotes:** a button's `order` and its visual's are independent and can **disagree** with nothing warning about it (validating needs a button→visual ownership relation, and `UiParent` is a *transform* hierarchy); and on events-only, the engine renders **nothing** — hover is visible only because the demo got a script, so "the UI feels dead" is half-fixed by the engine and half by a demonstration. **Writing the plan's Review Focus list found two edge cases that fell out of neither the spec nor the natural tests.** An absent pointer provider fell back to `PointerState{}` — which is `{0, 0, false}`, a *real* position inside any button anchored `TopLeft` at `{0,0}`, and the test harness's own button is exactly that — so hover would have emitted a phantom enter for a pointer that does not exist; harmless for clicks, since no press ever arrives. And a hovered entity that loses its `UiButton` has no channel name left to emit `hover_exit` on, because the name lived on the component: it emits nothing but still clears `hovered_`, or the next genuine hover would be mistaken for "no change" and swallowed. **The spec's one open risk is now measured and answered.** Hover does **not** stick when the pointer leaves the window — the GLFW provider clamps the cursor to `[0,1]` (`app/main.cpp:301-314`), so it tracks to the window **edge** instead. Still wrong for a button flush to that edge, which stays hovered while the pointer is over another application; the demo's bar starts at x = 0.02 so it is unaffected, which is also why eyes would not catch it. Backlogged as a **provider** fix (a `GLFW_HOVERED`-backed "inside the window" flag, the same `PointerState` widening U9 needs) rather than a timeout inside `UiSystem`, which would make hover depend on frame timing. **Probes: 12 of 12 red.** One of the spec's eight named probes did not apply — `std::sort` versus `stable_sort` — because `topmost_hit` deliberately does not sort: it is a single O(n) pass where the equal-order rule is one documented character (`>=`), so probe 1 (`>=` to `>`) is the direct equivalent. **Two things the plan got wrong, both caught by running it:** eight assertions across `ui_system_test.cpp` and `windowed_systems_test.cpp` counted *every* event on a button's channel as a proxy for "a click happened", and hover now shares that channel **under the same name** — so the count cannot even be filtered by name; they now assert the click specifically, which is sharper. And the demo's own `hud_click` logger checked only `e.name`, so it would have logged "HUD button clicked" on **every pointer crossing** — a name-only test is no longer sufficient anywhere. **Gate:** 1234/1234, tier rules and the test-name checker clean, and both `mosaic run --window` and `mosaic editor` exit 0. **One Critical and six Important findings came back from the whole-branch review, all fixed in one pass.** The sharpest: the `if (!pointer_)` guard closed the phantom-hover hole for an EMPTY provider and I never extended the reasoning to providers that return a ZEROED one — and every real provider spelled "no pointer" as exactly the `{0,0,false}` the guard was written to reject. Headless `mosaic run` never creates a GLFW window, so it reported `{0,0}` every tick and would hover any button whose rect contains the origin **forever** — which a default `UiButton` (`TopLeft`, pos `{0,0}`, size `{0.1,0.1}`) does; the demo escaped only because its bar starts at x = 0.02. In the editor, `pointer_state` gated only `down` on `hovered`, so moving the cursor onto the Inspector kept reporting a position clamped to the viewport edge and latched an edge-anchored button lit with no `hover_exit` ever firing. Fixed by giving `PointerState` an explicit `inside` flag honoured in `topmost_hit`, gating the editor provider on the `hovered` signal **that already existed** for `down`, and giving headless no provider at all rather than one that returns zeroes. My own backlog note had deferred part of this claiming the fix needed a U9-style widening — that was wrong for the editor path, where the signal was already sampled three lines away. **Also corrected: this file and the spec both recorded a FALSE reason for hover being inert in a stopped editor.** It is not the play-mode gate — `pointer_state` never gates position — it is that `editor.cpp` calls `loop.stop()`, so `UiSystem::update` never runs. That matters because the recorded reason is what a maintainer acts on: anything that makes the editor tick while stopped (an authoring-time system, or a fix for `editor --ticks`) turns hover on in authoring mode, where in this demo pointer movement writes `panel->alpha` into a scene the user may save. **Outstanding, needs a human:** with `mosaic run --window`, moving the pointer over the HUD bar should brighten it and moving away should dim it; in `mosaic editor` it does nothing until **Play**, because the loop is stopped. **Next:** capture/drag is the remaining third of U6; 9-slice is unblocked but deliberately deferred as a longer-term goal; the two accepted costs above likely want solving together, since both need a UI ownership definition. The standing open question is unchanged — whether UI is a near-term goal at all.)

**Last updated:** 2026-10-10 (**UI — U7 image presentation shipped, two of its three features: `UiImage` sub-rects and Stretch/Fit/Fill aspect modes. 9-slice was split out at the START of the slice, not dropped from it.** The three features U7 grouped are not independent — 9-slice needs nine sub-rects of one texture, so it is built *on top of* this UV plumbing, and bundling would have put the plumbing, an aspect enum and a **nine-fold draw-count change** in one diff where a reviewer would have to find the performance dimension amongst two unrelated features. **Three of the four scoping findings changed the design rather than confirming it.** The sub-rect mechanism was not new design at all: `UiTextPush` has carried a `uv[4]` and `ui_text.vert` the exact `mix()` line since the text slice, so `UiImagePush` 32 → 48 and `ui_image.vert` are a near-copy of a shipped pattern. `TextureCache` already loaded the `Image` to call `create_texture` and then **threw the dimensions away**, so it now keeps `{handle, width, height}` and nothing new is loaded. And `RenderSystem`'s existing `if (tex == 0) return` turned "what if the dimensions are unknown" from a fallback to invent into an **invariant to pin**: a non-zero handle implies non-zero dimensions, recorded only alongside a successful upload, so no caller needs an unknown-dimensions branch and no frame can show the whole atlas while waiting for a load. **The arithmetic is CPU-side behind a pure function** (`engine/ui/ui_fit.hpp`'s `resolve_image_quad`), and that was a close call — roughly 75/25, recorded as such in the spec. The shader could call `textureSize` and get the dimensions for free, and aspect correction is rasterization-adjacent. Three things decided it: the failure mode (using one of the two aspects) produces a **plausible** image rather than a broken one, so neither review nor eyeballing catches it and only a unit test can; `layout_text` already takes `aspect_` and corrects on the CPU, so this follows precedent rather than preference; and `textureSize` saves nothing, because a **pixel** sub-rect needs the dimensions on the CPU regardless. **The condition for revisiting, stated now so it is checkable later:** if the correction ever needs **per-pixel** information — tiling within a sub-rect, or a shader-side 9-slice — the shader is right and this seam becomes a dead end; today the correction is per-quad. Sub-rects are authored in **texture pixels**, not normalized UVs, because that is how atlas art is authored and how any tool reports it, and hand-divided UVs break silently when an atlas is repacked. **BOTH aspects are load-bearing and every aspect test uses a non-square screen deliberately** — a normalized rect is not square, so the correction reconciles the texture's `w/h` against `(rect.w * screen_aspect) / rect.h`, and a case on a square screen would pass with the screen-aspect term dropped. **The probes earned their keep: 11 of 12 red, and the one that was not found a real gap.** Recording `width`/`height` even when `handle == 0` left the whole suite green, because the only miss the tests exercised was an *unregistered* id — which never loads an `Image`, so the guarded code never runs. The case that exercises it is an `Image` that loads and then fails to upload; that test now exists and the probe is red. Two other things worth knowing: the plan's own worked examples were executed against a reference implementation rather than trusted, which caught a test case that would have gone **red against correct code** (a 50x100 texture in a `{0.2, 0.2}` rect at screen aspect 0.5 has rect_aspect *equal* to the image aspect, so nothing letterboxes); and `app/run/demo_scene.hpp`'s **positional** `UiImage` init silently re-bound `tint` onto the first new field — gcc caught it only because `Vec3` does not convert to `Vec2`, so two same-typed neighbours would have compiled and rendered wrong. That init is now designated. **Gate:** 1203/1203 green, tier rules clean, `ui_image.vert` compiles to SPIR-V, and both `mosaic editor` and `mosaic run --window` exit 0 with zero validation output — which matters here rather than being ceremonial, since a push-constant range that disagreed with the shader's 48-byte block is exactly what Vulkan validation reports. **Visually CONFIRMED at the window on 2026-10-10**, which is the only instrument that could confirm it: the demo's top-right checker renders **square**, letterboxed inside its rect, rather than stretched into rectangles, and it **stays square as the window is resized**. That single observation is the whole slice, because it can only hold if BOTH the texture aspect and the screen aspect are live at runtime — a build using one of them would look correct at exactly one window size and wrong at every other. The unit tests pin the arithmetic and the contract tests pin the GLSL, but neither can execute the shader, so this closed the last gap rather than being a formality. **Next:** 9-slice is now unblocked and carries three decisions of its own; U6 interaction depth still waits on the `UiButton` `order` question; the editor-starts-stopped change still carries the `--ticks` trap. And the standing open question is unchanged — whether UI is a near-term goal at all.)

**Last updated:** 2026-10-10 (**Manual acceptance passed, and it produced a design decision — the editor should start STOPPED.** A human worked a 23-item checklist at the window and every item passed. That mattered rather than being a formality: the viewport-pointer branch review had proved the feature was *shipped dead* while all thirteen of its tests were green, so a person at the window was the only instrument that could confirm the fix. **What it confirmed, specifically, because none of it is reachable by an automated test here:** the HUD button logs `hud_click` on a play-mode click; the sphere, both boxes and the capsule each take a visible impulse while the static ground does not; clicks over the Inspector or Hierarchy never reach the scene; pressing Stop restores click-to-select and silences the button; a press held through a mode change clicks nothing until a fresh press; a slider drag released over the viewport shoves nothing; and coordinates stay true near the panel edges after a resize. Exposure was confirmed in the same pass — 1.0 unchanged, 2.0 brightening geometry **and** sky together, the unlit overlays correctly *not* following, and a negative value rendering **black rather than white**, which is the ACES floor fix holding. **The decision that came out of it.** Because `PlayState` defaults to `playing_ = true`, the editor begins simulating the moment it opens — so with viewport clicks routed to the game while playing, the default click now drives the game and selection requires pressing Stop first. The product is editor-facing, so the default should be authoring. **Deferred rather than done, with the trap written down:** it is one token (`playing_ = false`) plus one inverted test, but `mosaic editor --ticks N` **would never terminate**, because `FramePump` exits on `loop_.tick_count() >= ticks` while `RuntimeLoop::advance` returns early when stopped, so the counter never moves. CI is unaffected (its smoke test runs `mosaic run`), but `--ticks` is exactly how the editor is verified in a sandbox that cannot screenshot — which is how every editor change this month was checked. So it needs a decision, not just an edit: `--ticks` counting frames in the editor path, or implying play, or a separate frame limit. The backlog entry also names the consequences that are **correct and must not be "fixed" on sight** — the scene sitting still until Play, the Blender live-link staying quiet, the Step button becoming enabled — because "the editor looks broken, nothing moves" is the obvious wrong conclusion. **Handoff state: nothing is outstanding.** Eight PRs merged across 2026-10-09, main green at 1183/1183, both `--window` entry points clean, and the repository and build verified intact after a power loss. Both 🟢 tiers and the sole 🟡 are struck, so the next pick comes from 🔴 — in order of how little it needs from a human: **U7 image presentation** is independent and needs no decision, **U6 interaction depth** is first in the UI decomposition's own ordering but carries the `UiButton` `order` question, and **U10** is blocked on an editor keyboard-input provider. Whether UI is a near-term goal at all is still unanswered, and the decomposition deliberately does not answer it.)

**Last updated:** 2026-10-09 (**Session close — six slices merged, the actionable tiers are empty, and one recorded item was withdrawn rather than built.** Shipped: multi-scatter energy compensation (#225), exposure control (#226), a 60s suite-wide ctest `TIMEOUT` (#227), viewport pointer input (#228), the UI pillar decomposition (#229), a fix for the flaky threaded-raycast test (#230), and a test-name lint (#231). **Both 🟢 quick wins and the sole 🟡 item are now struck**, so the next pick comes from the 🔴 tier — where the PBR row is complete and the UI row is decomposed into U6–U11. **What a worker should pick up, in order of how little it needs from a human:** U7 image presentation (aspect-preserving fit/fill, 9-slice, atlas sub-rects) is independent, cheap and needs no decision; U6 interaction depth is first in the decomposition's own ordering but carries an open question — whether `UiButton` gets its own `order` or inherits its `UiPanel` ancestor's, where the recommendation is inherit-with-override; U10 is blocked on an editor keyboard-input provider, which is itself a recorded near-one-line item. **Whether UI is a near-term goal at all is still unanswered and is not the decomposition's to answer.** **Two outstanding things that need a human, not a worker** — both since resolved; see the 2026-10-10 entry above. Visual acceptance of viewport pointer input was genuinely load-bearing rather than a formality: the branch review proved that feature was *shipped dead* while all thirteen of its tests were green, so a person at the window was the only instrument that could confirm it. And the exposure slice's visual change was unconfirmed at the time of writing. **The most transferable lesson of the session is about tests rather than about rendering, and it recurred in three different shapes.** A guard can be green and guard nothing: because its probe set was derived from the assertion list rather than from the code (multi-scatter, which left one statement unpinned); because a textual pin asserts that a line *exists* and never that it is *correct* (the pointer slice, whose pin asserted the presence of the very `WantCaptureMouse` call that made the feature unreachable); and because a test can assert a *race outcome* (the physics raycast case, which failed on a 2-core runner despite every claim it makes being met). Each was found by a different instrument — mutation probes derived from code, a fresh-context review, and `taskset -c 0` reproducing 9/15 failures on demand — and none of the three would have been found by reading. **A fourth shape, worth equal weight: the right answer is sometimes not to do the recorded work.** The backlog said nine test names were unrunnable because of commas; there are 89, `CatchAddTests.cmake` escapes them, and the defect was in the probe scripts' own unescaped invocation. Renaming 89 well-written names would have been churn against a non-problem, so the item is withdrawn with the evidence and only the two constraints that genuinely break ctest discovery got a lint.)

**Last updated:** 2026-10-07 (**Studio — viewport pointer input shipped, play-mode only: the editor can finally click its own scene, and the three things that made this the backlog's only 🟡 were not the three things that turned out to be hard** — `mosaic editor` could select entities and edit components live, but a click in its viewport reached nothing: `setup_runtime`'s `pointer` parameter defaults to `{}` and `editor.cpp` never passed one, so `UiSystem` saw `PointerState{}` forever, and `ClickNudgeSystem` was never registered there at all — `register_windowed_systems` had exactly one caller, `main.cpp`. Manipulating a scene and exercising its scripts were two different programs. **The behaviour question the backlog flagged as blocking was answered first, as it should have been: play mode only.** A viewport click drives the game's pointer while playing and means "select this entity" while not. **Scoping then found the coordinate transform already existed** — `StudioApp::draw_viewport` has converted the mouse to panel-relative coordinates and raycast-picked on `IsItemClicked` since 37d, for edit-mode selection. That is what made play-mode-only cheap: the existing pick *is* the edit-mode behaviour and stays. But it had to be **gated on `!is_playing()`**, or one play-mode click both drives the pointer and moves the selection, so the Inspector jumps to whatever the user just poked. **That gate is a behaviour change to shipped code, not an addition:** clicking the viewport no longer selects while playing. Selection stays reachable from the Hierarchy panel, so nothing becomes unreachable. **The two findings scoping did not anticipate are the interesting ones.** `ClickNudgeSystem` fires on a press **edge** (`p.down && !was_down_`), so gating `down` on play mode *synthesises a click*: hold the left button in edit mode, press Play, and `down` flips false→true with no physical press, landing wherever the cursor happens to sit — and the same thing happens when a drag on an inspector slider is released over the viewport. `ViewportPanel` now latches "this press began while ineligible" and stays suppressed until the raw button goes up. And **the `aspect` provider must be the viewport panel's, not the window's**: the scene renders into a panel-sized offscreen target, so a window-derived aspect makes the nudge ray diverge from the image that was clicked — worse the further from centre, and wrong whenever the viewport is not the whole window, which is always. **The seam is `ViewportPanel`**, which was already headless, already unit-tested and already record-then-query for sizing; it now records the raw ImGui facts and computes the `PointerState`, so every suppression rule is tested with no window and no GPU. The one-frame latency is **inherited, not invented** — `on_frame` ticks the world before drawing the UI, the same ordering that already gives sizing its documented frame of lag. **A test-design subtlety worth carrying forward.** Testing each suppression condition by starting a press with that condition already unmet *cannot work*: the latch blocks such a press anyway, so dropping the condition from the gate leaves the test green. The conditions are tested as **press-then-change** instead — press legitimately, then remove one condition — which isolates the gate and happens to match the real scenarios (press in the viewport and drag off it; press and then hit Stop). The probes confirmed it: dropping any one of the three gate conditions turns that case red. **Eleven probes derived from the code, ten red.** The eleventh is recorded as a known gap rather than papered over: registering `ClickNudgeSystem` before `UiSystem` would break the UI-claim arbitration, and **no headless assertion in this slice can see it**, because registration order is a runtime property of `World`. Also honest about a limit the probes exposed: the end-to-end case that drives a real `UiButton` through a real `UiSystem` builds its *own* world, so it proves the mechanism works but **cannot** prove `editor.cpp` uses it — only the textual pin catches the provider being dropped. Neither alone is sufficient. **Two of my own test-authoring errors, from opposite directions, both caught by running them.** Searching raw source for `glfwGetCursorPos` matched the *comment explaining why it is not used* — exactly the trap `shader_source.hpp`'s `code_only` exists for, whose comment already said "a test that forbids documenting the decision it enforces is the wrong test" — and then, after stripping comments, a search for `/*windowed=*/true` could not match because stripping had removed it. Both pins are now comment-stripped and whitespace-normalised. **And the branch review found that the gate used the wrong ImGui signal, so the feature shipped DEAD — the single most valuable finding of the slice.** `down` required `hovered && !imgui_capturing`, with the second fed from `ImGui::GetIO().WantCaptureMouse`. That flag reads like "ImGui wants the mouse for a widget"; `imgui.cpp:5184` defines it as true whenever the cursor is over **any** ImGui window or any button is held. The Viewport panel *is* an ImGui window, and `IsItemHovered()` is false unless `g.HoveredWindow == window` (`imgui.cpp:4575`) — so the two conditions were **mutually exclusive** and `down` was unreachable. The latch had the identical defect, so fixing one would not have helped. **And because click-to-select is now gated on `!is_playing()`, the net effect was a regression rather than a no-op:** a play-mode viewport click did *nothing*, where before the slice it selected an entity. **Three things make this worth remembering.** No test of the shape chosen could ever have caught it — all thirteen feed the sample by hand, so the policy was right and thoroughly proven while its *input* was wrong. The textual pin made it worse by **asserting `WantCaptureMouse` was present**, cementing the bug; it is now inverted to *forbid* it, verified by reintroducing the call and watching the pin go red, and the general lesson is that a textual pin asserts a line exists and never that it is correct. And the fix **removes** the field rather than feeding it something else, because `IsItemHovered()` already encodes every case it was written for: another item active such as a slider drag elsewhere (`imgui.cpp:4581`, which excludes `MoveId`/`TabId` so a drag *inside* the viewport still counts), the viewport window behind another, and an open modal. Two further review findings fixed in the same pass: the end-to-end case asserted `press_consumed()` after one tick with `down` true, so the **release** edge that actually emits the event went untested — and its full-panel button made *every* position pass, so a provider returning (0,0) unconditionally would have been green; it now presses and releases, asserts the `ScriptEvent`, uses a centred sub-rect and has a negative-position case. And an undrawn Viewport panel (closed, collapsed, or an inactive dock tab) left the sample and the latch frozen, so draws resuming mid-press reused the previous press's verdict; all three paths now feed an empty sample, which makes the header's "sees every frame" claim true. Gate **1181/1181**; both `--window` entry points exit 0 with zero Vulkan validation lines. **Visually confirmed 2026-10-10** by a human working a 23-item checklist at the window, and it is worth saying what that confirmed because no automated test could: the HUD button logs `hud_click` on a play-mode click; the sphere, both boxes and the capsule each take a visible impulse while the static ground does not; clicks over the Inspector or Hierarchy never reach the scene; pressing Stop restores click-to-select and silences the button; a press held through a mode change clicks nothing until a fresh press; and a slider drag released over the viewport does not shove anything. Exposure was confirmed in the same pass — 1.0 unchanged, 2.0 brightening geometry *and* sky together, the unlit overlays correctly not following, and a negative value rendering **black rather than white**, which is the ACES floor fix holding. **The acceptance pass also produced a design change, which is the useful kind of outcome:** because the editor starts in play mode, the default viewport click now drives the game rather than selecting, and the user's judgement is that an editor-facing product should start **stopped**. Recorded in the backlog with its one real trap — `mosaic editor --ticks N` would never terminate, since `FramePump` counts world ticks and a stopped loop never ticks. Deferred and recorded: keyboard input to the scene from the editor (the same shape of gap, deliberately not bundled, since it needs none of this slice's decisions), extra mouse buttons in `PointerState`, and an editor fly-camera — whose real cost is that it breaks the current property that the user's click ray and the game's camera ray are identical by construction.)

**Last updated:** 2026-10-07 (**Rendering — exposure control shipped, and the backlog's own one-line description of it was wrong in the one way that mattered** — tone mapping had shipped with a hard-coded curve and no way to set the exposure feeding it. `Camera` gains `float exposure = 1.0f`, reflected, so it serialises with the scene, is per-camera, and arrives in the Studio inspector as a live `DragFloat` with no extra code; 1.0 renders byte-identically to before, which is the whole migration story. **The knob's location was the real work, exactly as the 2026-10-04 deferral predicted** — it went on `Camera` because exposure is a camera property physically and in every DCC tool. **But the backlog also said it was "one `float` through the existing lighting UBO (which already carries `camera_position`)", implying a free padding slot, and there is none:** IBL slice 4c took `camera_position.w` for its enable flag and left a comment warning that a future field claiming that slot would *silently disable image-based lighting*. This slice is that future field. It grew the UBO by a `vec4` instead, 192 → 208, and **appended it at the end rather than grouping it with `camera_position` where it belongs logically** — because `static_assert(sizeof(FrameLightingUbo) == 208)` checks the struct's **total size and never its field offsets**, so a mid-struct insertion updated on only one side of the C++/GLSL boundary would rebind every later member with the assertion still green, surfacing as a wrong shadow matrix rather than a failed build. A new contract case pins the GLSL block's member **order**, with its own honest limit recorded: it pins the GLSL side only, since the C++ struct is file-local to a `.cpp` in the backend and no test can include it. **The skybox needed a second route.** `skybox.frag` does not bind the per-frame UBO at all — its set 0 is the environment cubemap — so exposure reaches it through `SkyboxParams` and a push constant (72 → 76), and a test asserts the two routes carry *the same value* rather than each merely being present; drift there reads as "the sky doesn't match the scene", an authoring complaint rather than a bug report. Exposure applies **exactly where tone mapping applies**, which is not a coincidence to tidy up later: a shader with no curve has nothing to scale. The four unlit scene shaders stay untouched, recorded with the genuinely open question of whether `world_text` is UI or scene. **Eleven mutation probes, derived from the CODE and not from the assertion list** — the lesson the multi-scatter review produced, applied on the first slice after learning it, and it paid immediately: **three probes are caught only by tests that did not exist when the probe list was written.** Dropping `sky.exposure`, and replacing either GPU-payload copy with a literal, all passed the entire suite until two new cases were added for them. **Two defects in this slice's own work, both found by the probes rather than by reading.** Two new test names contained **commas**, which Catch2 treats as test-spec separators — so running either by name matched nothing and exited non-zero, producing two false-red columns across all eleven probes, the same trap that had already cost a full probe round on the previous slice. Renamed — though **this diagnosis was itself half wrong, corrected 2026-10-09**: the same sweep reported "nine pre-existing comma'd names" and the backlog recorded them as unrunnable. There are **89**, and they are **not broken** — `CatchAddTests.cmake` escapes commas when generating each `add_test`, so every one runs correctly under `ctest`. The defect was always in the probe scripts' *unescaped* per-name invocation, not in the test names; the entry is withdrawn and the surviving real constraints (a leading `--`, an unbalanced `[`) now have a lint instead. And the mid-block UBO probe inserted a *duplicate* member instead of moving one, so glslc rejected it and the probe tested nothing; corrected, it is now red on both the member-order pin and the vert/frag match test. A pre-existing guard also did real work: the case pinning that `cube.vert` and `cube.frag` declare matching `FrameLighting` blocks caught the first version of this change, which added the member to the fragment shader alone. Gate **1165/1165**; both `--window` entry points exit 0 with **zero Vulkan validation errors**, which is meaningful evidence here rather than a formality — a std140 mismatch between the grown buffer and the shader's declared block is exactly the kind of thing validation reports. Deferred and recorded: exposure on the four unlit shaders, EV-stop authoring units with a UI slider, and auto-exposure, which depends on the still-deferred offscreen HDR target.)

**Last updated:** 2026-10-06 (**Rendering — multi-scatter energy compensation shipped: the PBR arc's one recorded correctness gap is closed, and the test the slice existed to satisfy could not validate the constant the slice introduced** — single-scatter GGX drops the light that would have bounced between microfacets, and IBL slice 4c's furnace test had measured the loss precisely: a white surface in a uniform environment returned **0.786** at roughness 0.508 and **0.3085** at roughness 1. The Fdez-Agüera analytic fit puts it back: **1.000000 at every one of the 4096 LUT texels.** `energy_compensate` in `app/asset/ibl_bake.cpp` carries the math so CI can execute it with no GPU, mirrored in `cube.frag`, and the whole slice cost little to scope **because 4c had chosen to pin the measured shortfall with its reason rather than widen a tolerance until it hid** — the number to beat was already written down, which is what that decision was for. **The BRDF LUT is untouched**; the correction is applied on top at reconstruction time, because it depends on the per-material `F0` while the table is indexed by `(NdotV, roughness)` alone — so baking it in is impossible, not merely undesirable. That also means the single-scatter baseline case was **kept rather than rewritten**, and the before and after both stand in the suite. **The headline finding is about the test, and it was predicted while scoping rather than found in review.** At `F0 = 1` the compensated total cancels to `Ess + (1 − Ess)` *algebraically*, so the furnace returns exactly 1.0 for **any** value of `Favg` — `/21`, `/5` and a bare `1.0` are indistinguishable to it. Dropping to a dielectric does not help: `kD` is *defined* as the energy residual, so the three terms sum to 1 for any `Favg` whatsoever at albedo 1. Energy conservation is tautological here and cannot be the test. The cover is to ask for `FmsEms` **in isolation** — `diffuse_color = 0` makes `kD` vanish, so the returned diffuse weight *is* `FmsEms`, needing no extra field on the struct — pinned against independently computed references where a bare `Favg = 1` is **15× to 36×** out. That is the sixth instance in this arc of a guard that guards nothing; the only new thing is that it was anticipated. **Twelve mutation probes, twelve reds — and the branch review then found a hole all twelve were structurally incapable of seeing.** The GLSL contract pinned five of the six new statements in `cube.frag` and omitted the one that *computes* `FmsEms`: the multiple-scattering series sum, which is the entire formula the slice adds. `vec3 FmsEms = vec3(0.0);` keeps the identifier declared, leaves both pinned expressions textually intact, and turns `Favg` and `Ems` into unused locals that glslc accepts without a diagnostic — **reproduced, with all 1158 tests green and the correction fully disabled in the shipped renderer.** Since `energy_compensate` has no production caller, that contract file is the only thing standing behind the shader. **The reason the probes could not find it is the lesson:** the five GLSL probes were derived *from the assertion list*, so they could only ever test statements something already asserted. A probe set built that way measures how well the existing assertions resist mutation, never whether the assertions cover the code — so it comes back 5/5 red and tells you nothing about the gap. Probes need to be derived from the *code*, not from the tests. Also tightened: the `Favg` margin was 5e-5, which admitted a `/20` divisor (`FmsEms` drifts only 1.9e-5 to 4.4e-5) — a case whose stated purpose is pinning that constant was resolving it to ±1 in the divisor. Now 5e-6, with the derivation in the test; the floor is the **2.6e-6** gap between the independent reference integral and the real bake at the extreme corner texel, which is a limit of the reference rather than of the code, and is why a first attempt at 2e-6 came back red on correct code. **Two of the plan's own predictions were also wrong.** The plan claimed that moving `FmsEms` from the irradiance group onto the prefiltered radiance would produce **no red anywhere in C++**, and built the shader contract test's rationale on it. Three C++ cases catch it: the plan's reasoning — a uniform environment makes the irradiance and prefiltered maps identical — holds for an assertion on the **sum** of the two weights and fails for one on the **parts**, and those cases assert the parts. The contract test still earns its place by pinning the GLSL independently of a twin CI cannot execute, but it is not the lone guard it was advertised as. Likewise `FssEss → Ess` was predicted to fail the `Favg` case and does not, since at `f0 = 0.04` with `diffuse_color = 0` the diffuse weight is pure `FmsEms`. **Two smaller traps are worth knowing.** `(1.0 - metallic)` had to stay, moved onto `diffuseColor`: it is tempting to let the energy residual handle metals alone, since `F0 = albedo` makes `kD` zero for free — but only where `albedo` is **1**, so a dark conductor (`albedo = 0.5`) would grow a diffuse lobe. And **the handoff this slice started from quoted the pre-4c buggy curve** — 0.896 at roughness 0.5 and 0.656 at 0.76 are what the wrong `k = alpha^2/2` remap produced, not the corrected integral — while `ibl_bake.hpp`'s own header comment was still documenting that bug *as the design*, against an implementation whose comment explains at length why it was wrong. Both corrected here. **Also learned the hard way:** a comma in a Catch2 `TEST_CASE` name makes the filter split on it, match nothing, and exit non-zero — indistinguishable from a failing test, and it cost a full round of seven mutation probes. Two case names lost their commas. Gate **1158/1158** (1151 before this slice), 23.4s, no exclusions; both `--window` entry points exit 0 with zero Vulkan validation errors and "image-based lighting active (5 prefiltered levels)" confirmed. **And the slice's own stated visual expectation was wrong, found by computing the magnitudes rather than by looking.** Every doc here, inherited from the backlog's original wording, said "a rough *dielectric* comes out darker than it should" and named the demo sphere's rough bands as where to see it — while the demo sphere is a **metal**, so the sentence contradicted itself. **The energy loss scales with the specular weight, which scales with `F0`:** a dielectric has `F0 = 0.04`, so specular is ~4% of its response and the diffuse lobe, which never lost energy, carries the other 96%. Measured against the demo scene, the ground, both boxes and the capsule change by **0.998× to 1.022×** — invisible — while the metal sphere's red channel gains **1.47× at roughness 0.75 and 1.95× at roughness 1**. The furnace test's dramatic 0.31 is measured at `F0 = 1`, which is a metal. **So the visible result is the one prior slices kept apologising for:** the metal sphere's rough bands go from near-black to a visible dark red glow, because `FmsEms` rides the irradiance and reaches conductors, which had received *zero* ambient before (`kD` is exactly 0 at `metallic = 1`). The glow is strongly red rather than grey, since multi-bounce energy is tinted by `F0` repeatedly — red gains 0.257 against green's 0.011. Smooth surfaces of any kind are unchanged, `Ems = 0` at roughness 0 making the correction identically zero, so a reviewer looking at the smooth end will correctly see nothing. **Visually confirmed 2026-10-06** by a human running `mosaic editor` against an eight-item checklist: the rough bands glowing red rather than near-black, the mirror band unchanged, the gradient free of new seams, the dielectrics unchanged, the skybox unchanged, and no NaN/black pixels at silhouette edges (the one place `cube.frag` divides without a guard). `demo_scene.hpp`'s "EXPECTED, NOT A BUG: this sphere reads near-BLACK" block was corrected too — it had been stale since 4b/4c (it still described the flat `0.1` ambient and told the reader IBL was what would eventually help) and this slice makes it wrong in the other direction as well. Deferred and recorded: the same compensation for the **direct** lighting path, which the furnace test structurally cannot measure because `brdf()` is shader code — it depends on either a C++ mirror of `brdf()` or GPU-capable CI.)

**Last updated:** 2026-10-06 (**Rendering — IBL slice 4c shipped: the PBR arc is complete, and the furnace test it unlocked immediately found a Critical bug in its own BRDF table** — the flat `0.1` ambient is replaced by a diffuse irradiance lookup and a specular prefiltered-environment lookup combined through the split-sum approximation. Both environment products are convolved **on the CPU at import**, the same decision 4b made for equirect-to-cube and for the same reason: there is no GPU in CI, so shader-side integrals could not be checked at all. **That decision is what made the furnace test possible, and the furnace test is the point of the slice.** PBR slice 2's spec recorded as its honest limit that *no test there could show the BRDF is correct, only that it is wired* — and named the furnace test as what would. It now exists: a white surface in a uniform environment must return exactly the light it receives, and it runs in CI because the integrals are C++. **It found the known limitation, quantified:** single-scatter GGX returns **0.896** at roughness 0.5, **0.656** at 0.76 and **0.31** at roughness 1 — losing about two thirds at the top end. Those numbers are pinned with their reason rather than hidden behind a widened tolerance, so when multi-scatter compensation lands the improvement is checkable against a recorded baseline; the backlog entry points at them. **And the branch review found that the BRDF table itself was wrong.** The Smith geometry remap was `k = alpha^2/2` where it must be `alpha/2` — the reference writes `float a = roughness; k = (a*a)/2`, whose `a` is *roughness*, while this code's was already alpha, so it squared twice. **The more instructive half is that the symptom had already been seen and mis-explained.** A 4.1x energy overshoot at grazing angles was diagnosed as sampling divergence (g_vis divides by NdotV) and renormalised away. It converges — 4.16x at 262,144 samples, verified independently before acting. The renormalisation was masking a real integration error, and in masking it clamped **21% of the table flat**: a hard white rim on every silhouette, identical at every roughness, where true values span 0.60-0.89. With the correct remap the grid maximum is exactly 1.0, reached only at roughness 0 where it is analytically right, so the renormalisation was deleted rather than kept. **Worse, the test added to catch exactly this was pinning the wrong value with its labels inverted** — fixing the code would have produced a red test blaming the fix. **The recurring lesson got its sharpest instance yet.** Across this slice six separate guards turned out to guard nothing, each found by mutation rather than by reading: the prefilter tests passed with NO BLUR AT ALL; the wrong geometry remap passed because every assertion sat at roughness 0 and 1, where the two remaps coincide; pinning the split-sum reconstruction did not pin the LUT lookup, so a constant passed while discarding the entire energy correction; the production level count had nothing tying it to the shader's `roughness * 4.0`; and `R = reflect(-v, n)` was unpinned, so `R = n` — the sphere reflecting whatever is directly overhead — left every test green.
